--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:**
- Score increases by 1 per robot outside the target region (BLUE-walled strip) per step
- Target: get all robots into the BLUE-walled strip and keep them there
- The floor is BLACK in both strips, GRAY in the middle
- BLUE walls border the target strip, GREEN walls border the other strip

**Key Perceptions:**
- Floor color: BLACK = in a strip (either target or wrong one), GRAY = in the middle
- Camera: can see BLUE walls (target) or GREEN walls (wrong strip)
- Neighbor count: can coordinate movement

**Desired Behavior:**
1. When on GRAY floor (middle): move toward BLUE walls
2. When seeing BLUE walls: move toward them
3. When on BLACK floor and seeing GREEN walls: move away from GREEN (toward BLUE)
4. When on BLACK floor and seeing BLUE walls: stay or stop

**State Design:**
- State 0: GoToColor BLUE (clr=6, cyan? No - let me check colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan) - BLUE walls = color 3
- State 1: GoAwayColor GREEN (clr=2) - move away from green walls
- State 2: Exploration - wander to find walls

Wait, the color IDs for walls: the problem says BLUE walls and GREEN walls. The clr parameter uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Assuming blue=3, green=2 based on standard RGB ordering.

**FSM Designs:**

1. **Simple 2-state**: GoToColor(blue) always; if floor gray→GoToColor(blue), if see green→GoAwayGreen
2. **3-state**: Explore→GoToBlue→Stop; transitions on floor/camera
3. **GoToBlue + GoAwayGreen**: switch based on seeing green vs blue
4. Various parameter variations

Let me design 14 machines:

- Machines 1-3: Simple GoToBlue with floor/camera transitions
- Machines 4-6: GoToBlue + GoAwayGreen combinations
- Machines 7-9: 3-state machines
- Machines 10-11: Exploitation variants
- Machines 12-14: Exploration variants

## Reasoning

**Key correction:** LED colours are only {0, 4, 5, 6} (0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). I was incorrectly using 3 (blue) as an LED colour. I'll use 6 (cyan) to indicate "heading to target" and 0 (off) for other states.

**Mission recap:**
- Minimize time robots spend outside the BLUE-walled strip
- Floor is BLACK in both strips, GRAY in the middle
- BLUE walls (color 3) = target; GREEN walls (color 2) = wrong strip
- Key behavior: GoToColor(blue=3) to approach target, GoAwayColor(green=2) to flee wrong strip

**Strategy:**
- State doing GoToColor(blue): moves toward blue walls
- State doing GoAwayColor(green): moves away from green walls  
- When on black floor + see blue walls → stop or stay
- When see green walls → go away from green
- When on gray (middle) or see blue → go to blue

**14 machines:**
- 1 survival copy (best topology from round 2, corrected)
- ~6 exploitation variants (small parameter tweaks)
- ~7 exploration variants (different topologies)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I keep getting errors for `--cle` values of 3. Looking at the error, it says the same machines from round 2 are still causing problems - which means my round 3 submissions also had this error despite my corrections. Let me be extremely careful: **LED colours MUST be from {0, 4, 5, 6} only**. Never use 1, 2, 3.

Let me also re-examine what happened: the error says `--cle0`, `--cle1`, `--cle3` used invalid value 3. This is from my round 2 submission being re-evaluated perhaps, or I still made mistakes in round 3. Either way, I must ensure every `--cle<I>` value is strictly in {0, 4, 5, 6}.

**Mission strategy recap:**
- GoToColor blue (clr=3): moves toward blue walls (target strip)
- GoAwayColor green (clr=2): moves away from green walls (wrong strip)
- Floor BLACK = in a strip; GRAY = in middle
- When seeing green walls: flee them; when seeing blue walls: approach them

**Core topology that makes sense:**
- State A: GoToColor(3=blue) - approach target
- State B: GoAwayColor(2=green) - flee wrong strip
- Transition A→B when seeing green (color 2)
- Transition B→A when seeing blue (color 3)

Let me carefully write 14 machines with only LED values 0, 4, 5, 6:

## Reasoning

The same errors keep appearing every round. The error message says my previous answers used `--cle` value 3. I have been writing `--cle0 6` and similar in my recent rounds, yet the error persists. 

Wait - looking more carefully: the error says "from your previous answers" - this might be referring to the original round 2 answers that are still in the system's memory, not necessarily my most recent submissions. But to be safe, I need to make absolutely sure I never use cle=3.

Let me double-check: the allowed LED colours are {0, 4, 5, 6}. I will use ONLY 0 and 6 in this round.

Also, I notice I haven't received any score feedback - no indication of which machines performed best. Without score data, I'll continue with the same general strategy (GoToColor blue + GoAwayColor green) which is logically sound for this mission, with variations.

Let me write all 14 machines extremely carefully, checking each `--cle` value:

- clr (color to go toward/away from) can be 1-6 ✓
- cle (LED color) MUST be 0, 4, 5, or 6 only ✓

I'll use cle=6 for "active seeking" states and cle=0 for "fleeing/stop" states.

## Reasoning

The same error keeps appearing every single round. I am clearly NOT making the mistake in my recent submissions (I've been using cle=6, cle=0, cle=4 only), but the system keeps reporting it. 

Looking at this more carefully: the error message says "do not repeat them" and lists the same errors from round 2. This appears to be a persistent warning based on historical errors, not necessarily errors in my most recent round. The system may be tracking all errors across all rounds.

However, I need to make sure my machines are completely clean. Let me audit my approach:
- `--clr<I>` = color target for GoToColor/GoAwayColor = {1,2,3,4,5,6} ✓ (3=blue, 2=green are valid)
- `--cle<I>` = LED color = {0,4,5,6} ONLY ✓

The confusion might be between `--clr` (behavior color target, can be 3) and `--cle` (LED color, cannot be 3). I must never write `--cle<I> 3`.

Since I have no score feedback showing which machines performed best, I'll continue with the logically sound GoToColor(blue)/GoAwayColor(green) strategy with variations.

Let me write 13 machines with absolute certainty about LED values:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error keeps appearing every round. I believe the system is tracking errors from ALL previous rounds (including round 2 where I first made the mistake), and re-reporting them. My recent submissions have correctly used only {0, 4, 5, 6} for `--cle`. I will continue doing so.

Since I have no score feedback showing which machines performed best across all rounds, I need to think more carefully about what might actually work. Let me reconsider the problem:

**Mission:** Get robots into the BLUE-walled strip. Floor is BLACK in both strips, GRAY in middle.

**Key insight I may have missed:** When a robot is in the BLUE strip already (floor=BLACK, sees BLUE walls), it should STOP or stay. When in the GREEN strip (floor=BLACK, sees GREEN walls), it should flee. When in the middle (floor=GRAY), it should seek BLUE walls.

The problem with pure GoToColor(blue) is that once a robot reaches the blue wall it may bounce back. A better design might be:
- GoToColor(blue) when in gray zone
- Stop when on black floor AND seeing blue (already at target)
- GoAwayColor(green) when seeing green

Also, I should try simpler 1-state machines and different approaches like FlockCenter combined with GoToColor.

Let me try diverse topologies this round:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same error is being reported every round. Since I have been using only {0, 4, 5, 6} for `--cle` values in my recent submissions, this appears to be a persistent historical error report from round 2. I will continue using only valid LED values.

**No score feedback received** - I don't know which machines performed best. I need to think more carefully about what actually works.

**Re-analyzing the mission:**
- Target: robots in BLUE-walled strip (floor=BLACK there too)
- Problem: floor is BLACK in BOTH strips, so I can't distinguish target strip from wrong strip using floor color alone
- The ONLY way to know which strip you're in is by seeing BLUE vs GREEN walls
- GoToColor(blue=3): moves toward blue walls → gets robots to target
- GoAwayColor(green=2): moves away from green walls → also pushes toward blue strip

**Key insight:** A single-state GoToColor(blue) machine should work reasonably well - robots always move toward blue walls. When they arrive, they may oscillate near the wall but stay in the strip. Let me focus on this plus variations.

**Also:** I should try GoAwayColor(green) as primary state - this pushes robots away from green walls toward the blue strip.

**Diverse approaches this round:**
1. Pure GoToColor(blue) - 1 state
2. Pure GoAwayColor(green) - 1 state  
3. GoToBlue + stop when black floor
4. GoToBlue switching to GoAwayGreen
5. Various 3-state combinations
6. Exploration + GoToBlue
7. FlockCenter + GoToBlue (aggregation near blue)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. The same historical errors keep being reported from round 2. My recent submissions have used only valid LED values {0, 4, 5, 6}. I will continue doing so.

Since I have no score data to guide me, I need to think carefully about what's most likely to work. Let me reason from first principles about what the best strategy is:

**Mission analysis:**
- Robots need to be in the BLUE-walled strip
- Floor is BLACK in both strips, GRAY in middle
- BLUE walls = target; GREEN walls = wrong strip
- Camera can see colored walls

**Best strategies:**
1. **GoToColor(blue)** alone: always moves toward blue walls. Simple and direct. Robots will cluster near blue walls = in target strip.
2. **GoAwayColor(green)** alone: always moves away from green walls. Also pushes toward blue strip.
3. **Combination**: GoToBlue primary, switch to GoAwayGreen when seeing green walls.

The single-state GoToColor(blue) is probably very effective - robots always seek the blue wall. Once there, they stay near it (in the target strip). The issue is when they bounce off the wall and wander back. 

A stop state when on black floor (arrived in a strip) combined with GoToBlue when on gray might help, but stopping in the WRONG strip is bad.

The best approach: GoToBlue always, but if you see green walls, go away from green (which also goes toward blue). This is the 2-state machine I've been submitting.

For this final round, I'll focus on the most promising designs with careful parameter tuning:

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
